is the Plancherel measure on
{\widehat{G}}
, see Section
2.1
.
The group Fourier transform yields an isomorphism between the Hilbert spaces
L^{2}(G\times{\widehat{G}})
and
L^{2}(G\times G)
since
\mathcal{F}_{G}^{-1}\sigma(x,\cdot)=\kappa_{x}
.
By the Plancherel formula, we obtain a more pleasing writing for the right-hand side in (
5.3
):

\|\sigma\|_{L^{2}(G\times{\widehat{G}})}=\|\kappa\|_{L^{2}(G\times G)}.

Naturally
\mathcal{A}_{0}(G)\subset L^{2}(G\times{\widehat{G}})
,
and we have a similar definition and properties for
L^{2}(M\times{\widehat{G}})
.

By homogeneity of nilmanifolds, the above properties also hold
[
Fis22c
]
for
\mathcal{A}_{0}(M)

asymptotically:

Proposition 5.13
.

Let
\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0}(M)
with associated kernel
\kappa_{\dot{x}}(z)
.

(1)

The integral kernel
K^{(\varepsilon)}
of
{\rm Op}^{(\varepsilon)}(\sigma)
is smooth on
M\times M
and satisfies for
\varepsilon
small:

\forall\dot{x}\in M\qquad K^{(\varepsilon)}(\dot{x},\dot{x})=\varepsilon^{-Q}\kappa_{\dot{x}}(0)\ +\ O(\varepsilon^{N}),

for any
N\in\mathbb{N}
, with

\kappa_{\dot{x}}(0)=\int_{{\widehat{G}}}{\rm Tr}\left(\sigma(\dot{x},\pi)\right)d\mu(\pi).

(2)

The operator
{\rm Op}^{(\varepsilon)}(\sigma)
is trace-class on
L^{2}(M)
with

{\rm Tr}\left({\rm Op}_{M}^{(\varepsilon)}(\sigma)\right)=\varepsilon^{-Q}\int_{M}\kappa_{\dot{x}}(0)d\dot{x}\ +\ O(\varepsilon^{N}),

for any
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
with

\int_{M}\kappa_{\dot{x}}(0)d\dot{x}=\iint_{M\times{\widehat{G}}}{\rm Tr}\left(\sigma(\dot{x},\pi)\right)d\dot{x}d\mu(\pi).

(3)

The operator
{\rm Op}^{(\varepsilon)}(\sigma)
is Hilbert-Schmidt on
L^{2}(M)
with

\|{\rm Op}_{M}^{(\varepsilon)}(\sigma)\|_{HS(L^{2}(M))}^{2}=\varepsilon^{-Q}\|\sigma\|_{L^{2}(M\times{\widehat{G}})}^{2}\ +\ O(\varepsilon).

Above, the implicit constants are bounded, up to constants
depending on
G,\Gamma
and possibly
N
, by some continuous semi-norms of
S^{-\infty}(M\times{\widehat{G}})
in
\sigma
.

6.
Semiclassical functional calculus on
G
and
M

In this section, we develop semiclassical functional calculi inside
\Psi^{\infty}(G)
and
\Psi^{\infty}(M)
. The proofs will be mainly about estimates allowing for the routine arguments in symbolic pseudo-differential calculi. The precise setting and hypotheses are presented in the next section.

6.1.
The main result

6.1.1.
The hypotheses

Setting 6.1
.

We consider a semiclassical family of pseudo-differential operators
T(\varepsilon)\in\Psi^{m}(G)
,
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
, whose corresponding symbols

\sigma
admit a semiclassical expansion at scale
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
:

T(\varepsilon):={\rm Op}^{(\varepsilon)}_{G}(\sigma),\qquad\sigma\sim_{\varepsilon}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\varepsilon^{j}\,\sigma_{j}\quad\mbox{uniformly in}\ S^{m}(G\times{\widehat{G}}).

Moreover, the operators
T(\varepsilon)
and the principal symbols
\sigma_{0}
are non-negative (in the sense of
(
4.4
) and Definition
4.30
for each
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
):

\sigma_{0}\geq 0\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad T(\varepsilon)\geq 0.

We have a similar setting on
M
.

We make the following two further assumptions, firstly on the order being positive and secondly on the principal symbol
\sigma_{0}
and its invertibility:

Hypothesis 6.2
.

m>0
.

Hypothesis 6.3
.

For any
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
, there exists
C_{\gamma}>0
such that for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
, for almost all
(x,\pi)\in G\times{\widehat{G}}
, and any
v\in\mathcal{H}_{\pi}^{\infty}
, we have

(6.1)

\|\pi(\text{\rm I}+\mathcal{R})^{\frac{\gamma}{\nu}}(\text{\rm I}+\sigma_{0}(x,\pi))v\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\pi}}\geq C_{\gamma}\|\pi(\text{\rm I}+\mathcal{R})^{\frac{m+\gamma}{\nu}}v\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\pi}},

We have a similar hypothesis on
M
.

The above hypotheses imply by Theorem
4.28
and Corollary
4.29
that
T(\varepsilon)
is essentially self-adjoint (among other properties), and it will therefore make sense to define its functional calculus:

Lemma 6.4
.

Under Setting
6.1
and Hypothesis
6.3
on
G
,
each operator
T(\varepsilon)
admits a left-parametrix, satisfies sub-elliptic estimates and is hypoelliptic.
Assuming in addition Hypothesis
6.2
,

T(\varepsilon)
is also essentially self-adjoint on
\mathcal{S}(G)\subset L^{2}(G)
.

We have similar properties in the nilmanifold setting
M
, where furthermore,
T(\varepsilon)
has compact resolvent, its spectrum is a discrete subset of
[0,\infty)
and its eigenspaces are finite dimensional.

6.1.2.
Main example

Our main example where the above hypotheses are satisfied is the sub-Laplcian in horizontal divergence form perturbed with a potential:

Example 6.5
.

Let
\mathcal{L}_{A}
be a non-negative sub-Laplacian in horizontal divergence form on a stratified group
G
as in Section
4.9
.
We assume that it
satisfies the hypothesis of uniform ellipticity of Lemma
4.39
.
Let
V\in C_{l,b}^{\infty}(G)
be non-negative.
By Lemma
4.39
, the family of differential operators given (using the notation of Section
4.9
) by

\varepsilon^{2}(\mathcal{L}_{A}+V)={\rm Op}_{G}^{(\varepsilon)}(\sigma),\quad\sigma=\sigma_{0}+\varepsilon\sigma_{1}+\varepsilon^{2}V,\quad\varepsilon\in(0,1],

falls under Setting
6.1
on
G
and satisfies Hypothesis
6.2
with
m=2
and Hypothesis
6.3
for
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{L}_{I}
.

We have a similar property for a sub-Laplacian in horizontal divergence form on a stratified nilmanifold
M
as in Section
4.9

perturbed by a non-negative potential
V\in C^{\infty}(M)
.

We can generalise the example of the sub-Laplacian in horizontal divergence form above in the following way:

Example 6.6
.

Consider the operators

\mathcal{R}_{A}={\rm Op}_{G}(\sigma_{0}+\sigma_{1})\quad\mbox{or respectively}\ {\rm Op}_{M}(\sigma_{0}+\sigma_{1}),

from Examples

4.41
and
4.43
on
G
,
or
4.42
and
4.44
on
M
.
Let
V\in C_{l,b}^{\infty}(G)
be non-negative on
G
,
or let
V\in C^{\infty}(M)
be non-negative on
M
.
Then

\varepsilon^{\nu}(\mathcal{R}_{A}+V)
falls under Setting
6.1
on
G
and satisfies Hypothesis
6.2
with
m
being the homogeneous degree of
\mathcal{R}_{A}
, and Hypothesis
6.3
for
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}_{I}
.
We can replace
V
with other non-negative pseudo-differential terms of order
<\nu
.

6.1.3.
Statement of the main result

We can now state the main result of this paper.

Theorem 6.7
.

We consider Setting
6.1
and Hypotheses
6.2
and
6.3

on
G
.
For any
\psi\in\mathcal{G}^{m^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R})
with
m^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
,

\psi(T)
decomposes as

\psi(T)={\rm Op}^{(\varepsilon)}_{G}(s_{\psi})+R,

with
s_{\psi}=s_{\psi}(\varepsilon)
,
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
, uniformly in
S^{mm^{\prime}}(G\times{\widehat{G}})
and
R=R(\varepsilon)
,
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
, semiclassically smoothing on the Sobolev scale
(in the sense of Definition
5.9
).
Moreover,
s_{\psi}
admits a semiclassical expansion

s_{\psi}\sim_{\varepsilon}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\varepsilon^{k}\tau_{k}\quad\mbox{uniformly in}\ S^{mm^{\prime}}(G\times{\widehat{G}}),

with principal symbol

\tau_{0}=\psi(\sigma_{0})\in S^{mm^{\prime}}(G).

We have a similar property on
M
where furthermore
R=R(\varepsilon)
is semiclassically smoothing (in the sense of Definition
5.8
).

The fact mentioned in the statement that the principal symbol
\sigma_{0}
has a functional calculus in the symbol classes is a consequence of Theorem
4.32
.
The proof of Theorem
6.7
relies on the construction of a parametrix for
z-T
(see Section
6.2
below), it will be given in Section
6.3
.

6.2.
Parametrix for
z-T(\varepsilon)

Our strategy to study the functional calculus of
T=T(\varepsilon)
relies on explicit expressions for the right parametrix of
z-T
given as follows:

Lemma 6.8
.

We consider Setting
6.1
and Hypotheses
6.2
and
6.3

on
G
.

(1)

For any
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
,
we set

b_{0,z}:=(z-\sigma_{0})^{-1},

and recursively for
k=1,2,\ldots
,

b_{k,z}:=(z-\sigma_{0})^{-1}d_{k,z},\qquad d_{k,z}:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j+[\alpha]+l=k\\
l<k\end{subarray}}\Delta^{\alpha}\sigma_{j}\ X^{\alpha}b_{l,z}.

For each
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, this defines a symbol
b_{k,z}\in S^{-m-k}(G\times{\widehat{G}})
and a symbol
d_{k,z}\in S^{-k}(G\times{\widehat{G}})
.
Moreover,
for any semi-norms

\|\cdot\|_{S^{-m-k},a,b,c}
and
\|\cdot\|_{S^{-k},a,b,c}
, there exist constant a
C>0
and powers
p\in\mathbb{N}
such that we have for all
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}

\|b_{k,z}\|_{S^{-m-k},a,b,c}\leq C\left(1+\frac{1+|z|}{|\text{\rm Im}\,z|}\right)^{p+1},\qquad\|d_{k,z}\|_{S^{-k},a,b,c}\leq C\left(1+\frac{1+|z|}{|\text{\rm Im}\,z|}\right)^{p+1}.

(2)

We construct
the symbol
p_{z}
with asymptotics

p_{z}\sim_{\varepsilon}\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\varepsilon^{j}\,b_{j,z}\quad\mbox{uniformly in}\ S^{-m}(G\times{\widehat{G}})

following the ideas of Borel’s extension lemma (see e.g.
[
FR16
, Section 5.5.1]
).
Then for any
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and for any semi-norm
\|\cdot\|_{S^{m-(N+1)},a,b,c}
, there exist a constant
C>0
and a power
p\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\forall z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R},\ \forall\varepsilon\in(0,1]\qquad\|p_{z}-\sum_{j=0}^{N}\varepsilon^{j}\,b_{j,z}\|_{S^{m-(N+1)},a,b,c}\leq C\left(1+\frac{1+|z|}{|\text{\rm Im}\,z|}\right)^{p+1}\varepsilon^{N+1}

(3)

The operator

P_{z}:={\rm Op}_{G}^{(\varepsilon)}(p_{z})
is a left parametrix for
z-T
in the sense that

(z-T)P_{z}-\text{\rm I}\in\Psi^{-\infty}(G),

is smoothing.
Moreover, writing
R_{z}:={\rm Op}_{G}^{(\varepsilon)}(r_{z}):=(z-T)P_{z}
,
for any
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and for any semi-norm
\|\cdot\|_{S^{-(N+1)},a,b,c}
, there exist a constant
C>0
and a power
p\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\forall z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R},\ \forall\varepsilon\in(0,1]\qquad\varepsilon^{-(N+1)}\|r_{z}\|_{S^{-(N+1)},a,b,c}\leq C\left(1+\frac{1+|z|}{|\text{\rm Im}\,z|}\right)^{p+1}.

A similar property holds true on
M
.

Proof.

Let us prove Part (1) inductively using the property of composition of symbols (see Section
4.1.5
).
The seminorm estimates for
b_{0,z}

follows from the resolvent bounds in Proposition
4.33
together with Remark
4.34
(1).
Let
k_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
. The estimates for
b_{0,z}

imply that if suffices to prove the seminorm estimates for
d_{k_{0},z}
:

\displaystyle\|d_{k_{0},z}\|_{S^{-k_{0}},a,b,c}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j+[\alpha]+l=k_{0}\\
l<k_{0}\end{subarray}}\|\Delta^{\alpha}\sigma_{j}\ X^{\alpha}b_{l,z}\|_{S^{-k_{0}},a,b,c}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j+[\alpha]+l=k_{0}\\
l<k_{0}\end{subarray}}\|\Delta^{\alpha}\sigma_{j}\|_{S^{m-j-[\alpha]},a_{1},b_{1},c_{1}}\|X^{\alpha}b_{l,z}\|_{S^{-k_{0}-m+j+[\alpha]},a_{2},b_{2},c_{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{l=0}^{k_{0}-1}\|X^{\alpha}b_{l,z}\|_{S^{-m-l},a_{2},b_{2},c_{2}}.

Hence, the seminorm estimates for
b_{l,z}
,
l<k
, will imply the estimates for
d_{k,z}

and therefore for
b_{k,z}
.
This
concludes the proof of Part (1).

The construction of the parametrix symbol is classical,
and the semi-norm estimates in Part (2) then follow from the construction and the estimates in Part (1).
For Part (3), we fix
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
We write

(z-T)P_{z}=S_{N,z}+\varepsilon^{N+1}{\rm Op}_{G}^{(\varepsilon)}(r_{0,N,z}),

where

S_{N,z}:={\rm Op}^{(\varepsilon)}_{G}\big(z-\sum_{j=0}^{N}\varepsilon^{j}\sigma_{j}\big){\rm Op}^{(\varepsilon)}_{G}\big(\sum_{l=0}^{N}\varepsilon^{l}b_{l,z}\big)\in\Psi^{0}(G).

The asymptotic expansions for the symbols of
P_{z}
and
T
together with the properties of composition imply that
r_{0,N,z}\in S^{-(N+1)}(G\times{\widehat{G}})
.
Moreover, for any semi-norm
\|\cdot\|_{S^{-N},a,b,c}
, there exist a constant
C>0
and a power
p\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\forall z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R},\ \forall\varepsilon\in(0,1]\qquad\|r_{0,N,z}\|_{S^{-(N+1)},a,b,c}\leq C\left(1+\frac{1+|z|}{|\text{\rm Im}\,z|}\right)^{p+1}.

We now analyse the symbol of
S_{N,z}={\rm Op}^{(\varepsilon)}_{G}(s_{N,z})
:

\displaystyle s_{N,z}

\displaystyle=(z-\sum_{j=0}^{N}\varepsilon^{j}\sigma_{j})\diamond_{\varepsilon}(\sum_{l=0}^{N}\varepsilon^{l}b_{l,z})

\displaystyle=\big(z-\sigma_{0})\diamond_{\varepsilon}(\sum_{j=0}^{N}\varepsilon^{j}b_{j,z})\ -\ (\sum_{j=1}^{N}\varepsilon^{j}\sigma_{j})\diamond_{\varepsilon}(\sum_{l=0}^{N}\varepsilon^{l}b_{l,z})

\displaystyle=\sum_{[\alpha]+l\leq N}\varepsilon^{[\alpha]+l}\Delta^{\alpha}(z-\sigma_{0})\ X^{\alpha}b_{l,z}-\sum_{[\alpha]+j+l\leq N,j>0}\varepsilon^{[\alpha]+j+l}\Delta^{\alpha}\sigma_{j}\ X^{\alpha}b_{l,z}\ +\ \varepsilon^{N+1}r_{1,N,z},

by the composition properties of the calculus (see Theorem
5.7
),
with
r_{1,N,z}\in S^{-(N+1)}(G\times{\widehat{G}})
satisfying
a similar estimates as
r_{0,N,z}
above.
In the first sum of the right-hand side above, we observe

\sum_{[\alpha]+l\leq N}\varepsilon^{[\alpha]+l}\Delta^{\alpha}(z-\sigma_{0})\ X^{\alpha}b_{l,z}=(z-\sigma_{0})\sum_{k=0}^{N}\varepsilon^{k}b_{k,z}\ -\sum_{[\alpha]+l\leq N,[\alpha]>0}\varepsilon^{[\alpha]+l}\Delta^{\alpha}\sigma_{0}\ X^{\alpha}b_{l,z}.

This yields

\displaystyle s_{N,z}

\displaystyle=(z-\sigma_{0})\sum_{k=0}^{N}\varepsilon^{k}b_{k,z}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}[\alpha]+j+l\leq N\\
j=0\Rightarrow[\alpha]>0\end{subarray}}\varepsilon^{[\alpha]+j+l}\Delta^{\alpha}\sigma_{j}\ X^{\alpha}b_{l,z}\ +\ \varepsilon^{N+1}r_{1,N,z}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{N}\varepsilon^{k}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j+[\alpha]+l=k\\
l<k\end{subarray}}\Delta^{\alpha}\sigma_{j}\ X^{\alpha}b_{l,z}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}[\alpha]+j+l\leq N\\
j=0\Rightarrow[\alpha]>0\end{subarray}}\varepsilon^{[\alpha]+j+l}\Delta^{\alpha}\sigma_{j}\ X^{\alpha}b_{l,z}\ +\ \varepsilon^{N+1}r_{1,N,z}

having used the definition of
b_{k,z}
from Part (1).
We have obtained

s_{N,z}=\text{\rm I}+\varepsilon^{N+1}r_{1,N,z}
.
This concludes the proof of Part (3).
∎

It is possible to also follow
the classical construction for a left parametrix instead of right parametrix for
z-T

with similar property as the right one constructed in Lemma
6.8
.
However, the construction for the left one involves more complicated expressions, and as we can avoid using it, we will not present it here.

We observe that the existence of parametrices implies norm bounds on the true resolvent of
T
:

Lemma 6.9
.

We consider Setting
6.1
and Hypotheses
6.2
and
6.3
on
G
. Then
(z-T)^{-1}
is bounded
L^{2}_{s-m}(G)\to L^{2}_{s}(G)

semiclassically
\varepsilon
-uniformly for any
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
. More precisely, let us fix a positive Rockland operator
\mathcal{R}
of homogeneous degree
\nu
; for any
s\in\mathbb{R}
, there exist a constant
C>0
and an integer
p\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\displaystyle\forall z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R},\quad\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1]}\big\|(z-T)^{-1}\big\|_{L^{2}_{s,\varepsilon}(G)L^{2}_{s+m,\varepsilon}(G)}\leq C\left(1+\frac{1+|z|}{|\text{\rm Im}\,z|}\right)^{p+1}.

A similar property holds true on
M
.

Proof.

Let
s\in\mathbb{R}
.
By Lemma
6.8
,
(z-T)^{-1}=P_{z}-(z-T)^{-1}R_{z}
, so

\displaystyle\|(\text{\rm I}+\varepsilon^{\nu}\mathcal{R}_{G})^{\frac{s}{\nu}}(z-T)^{-1}(\text{\rm I}+\varepsilon^{\nu}\mathcal{R}_{G})^{-\frac{s-m}{\nu}}\|_{\mathscr{L}(L^{2}(G))}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\|(\text{\rm I}+\varepsilon^{\nu}\mathcal{R}_{G})^{\frac{s}{\nu}}P_{z}(\text{\rm I}+\varepsilon^{\nu}\mathcal{R}_{G})^{-\frac{s-m}{\nu}}\|_{\mathscr{L}(L^{2}(G))}+

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\|(\text{\rm I}+\varepsilon^{\nu}\mathcal{R}_{G})^{\frac{s}{\nu}}(z-T)^{-1}R_{z}(\text{\rm I}+\varepsilon^{\nu}\mathcal{R}_{G})^{-\frac{s-m}{\nu}}\|_{\mathscr{L}(L^{2}(G))}.

By the properties of the semiclassical calculus,
the first
\mathscr{L}(L^{2}(G))
-norm on the right-hand side is bounded up to a constant by a
S^{-m}
-semi-norm of
p_{z}
.
We now assume
s\leq 0
. Since
T
is essentially self-adjoint, the second
\mathscr{L}(L^{2}(G))
-norm is

\leq\frac{1}{|\text{\rm Im}\,z|}\|R_{z}(\text{\rm I}+\varepsilon^{\nu}\mathcal{R}_{G})^{-\frac{s-m}{\nu}}\|_{\mathscr{L}(L^{2}(G))}

Using again the properties of the semiclassical calculus, this last
\mathscr{L}(L^{2}(G))
-norm is bounded up to a constant by a
S^{(s-m)}
-semi-norm of
R_{z}
. The estimates for the semi-norms in
p_{z}
and
R_{z}
imply the result for
s\leq 0
.
We conclude the proof with an argument of duality and interpolation to obtain the case
s>0
.
∎

6.3.
Proof of the main result

In this section, we prove Theorem
6.7
.
The main step consists in adapting the arguments given in Section
4.8.2

regarding the functional properties of
\sigma_{0}
; we summarise them in the following statements:

Lemma 6.10
.

We continue with the setting of Theorem
6.7
.
We fix
m_{1}\leq 0
.
Let
a_{z}(x,\pi)
,
(x,\pi)\in G\times{\widehat{G}}
, be a field of bounded operators forming a symbol
a_{z}
in
S^{m_{1}}(G\times{\widehat{G}})
depending in
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
in such a way that
\bar{\partial}_{z}a_{z}=0
.
We also assume that for any seminorm

\|\cdot\|_{S^{m_{1}},a,b,c}
, there exist
C=C_{k,a,b,c}>0
and
p\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\forall z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}\qquad\|a_{z}\|_{S^{m_{1}},a,b,c}\leq C\left(1+\frac{1+|z|}{|\text{\rm Im}\,z|}\right)^{p}.

Let
\psi\in\mathcal{G}^{m^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R})
with (fixed)
m^{\prime}<-1
.
We consider an almost analytic extension
\tilde{\psi}
of
\psi
satisfying the following properties:

(1)

\tilde{\psi}
is supported in
\{x+iy\in\mathbb{C},|y|\leq 10(1+|x|)\}
,

(2)

(-i\partial_{y})^{p}\tilde{\psi}|_{\mathbb{R}}=\psi^{(p)}
for any
p\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

(3)

for any
p\in\mathbb{N}
,

\sup_{z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}}(1+|z|)^{p-m^{\prime}}|\partial_{y}^{p}\tilde{\psi}(z)|<\infty,

(4)

for any
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

\int_{\mathbb{C}}\big|\bar{\partial}\tilde{\psi}(z)\big|\left(\frac{1+|z|}{|\text{\rm Im}\,z|}\right)^{N}L(dz)<\infty,

Then the formula

\tau(a_{z},\psi):=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{C}}\bar{\partial}\tilde{\psi}(z)\ a_{z}\,L(dz),

defines a symbol
\tau(a_{z},\psi)
that is
\varepsilon
-uniformly in
S^{m_{1}}(G\times{\widehat{G}})
.
It is independent of the choice of such analytic extensions
\tilde{\psi}
, which exist by Proposition

B.1
.
Moreover, for any seminorm
\|\cdot\|_{S^{m_{1}},a,b,c}
, there exist
C>0
and
a seminorm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{G}^{m^{\prime}},N}
such that

\forall\psi\in\mathcal{G}^{m^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}),\ \forall t\in(0,1]\qquad\|\tau(a_{z},\psi(t\,\cdot))\|_{S^{m_{1}},a,b,c}\leq Ct^{-1}\|\psi\|_{\mathcal{G}^{m^{\prime}},N}.

Similar properties hold true on
M
.

Remark 6.11
.

The meaning of the dependence in
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
such that

\bar{\partial}_{z}a_{z}=0
is that for any
(x,\pi)\in G\times{\widehat{G}}
and
v_{1},v_{2}\in\mathcal{H}_{\pi}
, the map

z\mapsto(a_{z}(x,\pi)v_{1},v_{2})_{\mathcal{H}_{\pi}}
is smooth and holomorphic on
\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
. Moreover,
we observe that the hypotheses on
a_{z}
in Lemma
6.10
are satisfied by the following symbols constructed in Lemma
6.8
:

b_{k,z}
with
m_{1}=-m-k
,

d_{k,z}
with
m_{1}=-k
,

p_{z}
with
m_{1}=-m
and

p_{z}-\sum_{k=0}^{N}\varepsilon^{k}b_{k,z}
with
m_{1}=-m-N
.
Indeed,

b_{k,z}
is a (non-commutative but with
(z,\bar{z})
-constant coefficients) polynomial expression in
(z-\sigma_{0})^{-1}
and
X^{\beta}\Delta_{\alpha}\sigma_{0}
for some finite indices
\alpha,\beta
; consequently,
\bar{\partial}_{z}b_{k_{0},z}=0
.

Proof.

We start with observing that given a function
F\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{C})
satisfying for any
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

\int_{\mathbb{C}}\big|F(z)\big|\left(\frac{1+|z|}{|\text{\rm Im}\,z|}\right)^{N}L(dz)<\infty,

the formula

\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{C}}F(z)\ a_{z}\,L(dz),

defines a symbol
\varepsilon
-uniformly in
S^{m_{1}}(G\times{\widehat{G}})

with

\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{C}}|F(z)|\ \|a_{z}\|_{S^{m_{1}},a,b,c}\,L(dz)\leq\frac{C}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{C}}|F(z)|\left(1+\frac{1+|z|}{|\text{\rm Im}\,z|}\right)^{p}L(dz)<\infty.

This implies in particular that
\tau_{k}(a_{z},\psi)
is a well defined symbol
\varepsilon
-uniformly in
S^{m_{1}}(G\times{\widehat{G}})
.
To show that it depends on
\psi
and not on the choice of its almost analytic extension
\tilde{\psi}
with the stated properties, we adapt the argument of
[
Dav95
, Section 2]
.
For
\delta\in(0,1/4]
and
R\geq 1
, we set

\Omega_{\delta,R}:=\{z\in\mathbb{C},|\text{\rm Re}\,z|<R\ \mbox{and}\ \delta<|\text{\rm Im}\,z|\leq 20R\}.

Let
\tilde{\psi}_{1}
and
\tilde{\psi}_{2}
be two almost analytic extensions of
\psi
satisfying the properties of the statement.
Since
\bar{\partial}_{z}a_{z}=0
,
Stokes’ (or Green’s) theorem yields:

\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\Omega_{\delta,R}}\bar{\partial}(\tilde{\psi}_{1}-\tilde{\psi}_{2})(z)\ a_{z}\,L(dz)=-\frac{i}{2\pi}\int_{\partial\Omega_{\delta,R}}(\tilde{\psi}_{1}(z)-\tilde{\psi}_{2}(z))\ a_{z}\,dz,

and therefore

\displaystyle\big\|\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\Omega_{\delta,R}}\bar{\partial}(\tilde{\psi}_{1}-\tilde{\psi}_{2})(z)\ a_{z}\,L(dz)\big\|_{S^{m_{!}},a,b,c}\leq\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial\Omega_{\delta,R}}|\tilde{\psi}_{1}(z)-\tilde{\psi}_{2}(z)|\ \|a_{z}\|_{S^{m_{1}},a,b,c}dz

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim\int_{\partial\Omega_{\delta,R}}|\tilde{\psi}_{1}(z)-\tilde{\psi}_{2}(z)|\left(1+\frac{1+|z|}{|\text{\rm Im}\,z|}\right)^{p}dz\lesssim I_{1,\pm}+I_{2,\pm},

where

\displaystyle I_{1,\pm}

\displaystyle=\int_{x=-R}^{R}|\tilde{\psi}_{1}-\tilde{\psi}_{2}|(x\pm i\delta)\left(1+\frac{R}{\delta}\right)^{p}dx,

\displaystyle I_{2,\pm}

\displaystyle=\int_{\pm y\in(\delta,20R)}|\tilde{\psi}_{1}-\tilde{\psi}_{2}|(\pm R+iy)\left(1+\frac{R}{|y|}\right)^{p}dx;

the integrals on the remaining two edges
y=\pm R
,
-R\leq x\leq R
, vanish because of the support properties of
\tilde{\psi}_{1}
and
\tilde{\psi}_{2}
.
To estimate
I_{1,\pm}
and
I_{2,\pm}
, we perform a Taylor expansion of each
\tilde{\psi}_{j}
,
j=1,2
, about
y\sim 0
:

\psi_{j}(z)=\sum_{p_{1}\leq N}\frac{(iy)^{p_{1}}}{p_{1}!}[\partial_{y}^{p_{1}}\psi_{j}](x)+r_{N,j}(z),\quad z=x+iy,

with remainder

r_{N,j}(z):=\frac{(iy)^{N+1}}{N!}\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)^{N}[\partial_{y}^{N+1}\psi_{j}](x+isy)\,ds.

The hypotheses on
\tilde{\psi}_{j}
,
j=1,2
, imply the remainder estimates

|r_{N,j}(z)|\lesssim_{N,\tilde{\psi}}|y|^{N+1}\int_{0}^{1}(1+|x+isy|)^{-(N+1-m^{\prime})}ds,

as well as the Taylor series of
\tilde{\psi}_{1}(x+iy)
and
\tilde{\psi}_{2}(x+iy)
about
y\sim 0
being identical, so we have:

\tilde{\psi}_{1}(z)-\tilde{\psi}_{2}(z)=r_{N,1}(z)-r_{N,2}(z)\quad\mbox{for any}\ N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

We can now go back to estimating

\displaystyle I_{1,\pm}

\displaystyle\leq\left(1+\frac{R}{\delta}\right)^{p}\int_{x=-R}^{R}(|r_{N,1}|+|r_{N,2}|)(x\pm i\delta)dx\lesssim\delta^{N+1}\left(1+\frac{R}{\delta}\right)^{p}R,

\displaystyle I_{2,\pm}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\pm y\in(\delta,20R)}(|r_{N,1}|+|r_{N,2}|)(\pm R+iy)\left(1+\frac{R}{|y|}\right)^{p}dy

\displaystyle\lesssim R^{-(N+1-m^{\prime})}\int_{y=\delta}^{20R}\left(y^{N+1}+R^{p}y^{N+1-p}\right)dy\lesssim R^{m^{\prime}+1},

when
N\geq p
.
Choosing
R=\delta^{-1}
and
N=p+1
yields

\lim_{\delta\to 0}I_{1,\pm}=\lim_{\delta\to 0}I_{2,\pm}=0,\quad\mbox{so}\ \lim_{\delta\to 0}\big\|\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\Omega_{\delta,R}}\bar{\partial}(\tilde{\psi}_{1}-\tilde{\psi}_{2})(z)\ a_{z}\,L(dz)\big\|_{S^{m_{1}},a,b,c}=0,

since
m^{\prime}<-1
.
By Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem, this implies:

\big\|\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{C}}\bar{\partial}(\tilde{\psi}_{1}-\tilde{\psi}_{2})(z)\ a_{z}\,L(dz)\tau(a_{z},\psi)\big\|_{S^{m_{1}},a,b,c}=0,

for any
a,b,c
. This shows that
\tau(a_{z},\psi)
is a well defined symbol in
S^{m_{1}}(G\times{\widehat{G}})
independently of the choice of analytic extensions
\tilde{\psi}
satisfying the stated properties.
We may as well choose the almost analytic extension
\tilde{\psi}
constructed in Proposition

B.1
.

We observe that if
\tilde{\psi}(z)
is an extension constructed in Proposition

B.1
, then
\tilde{\psi}(tz)

is an almost analytic extension for
\psi(t\lambda)
satisfying the properties required in the statement, so we have

\tau(a_{z},\psi(t\,\cdot))=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{C}}t(\bar{\partial}\tilde{\psi})(tz)\ a_{z}\,L(dz)=\frac{t^{-1}}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{C}}\bar{\partial}\tilde{\psi}(z)\ a_{t^{-1}z}\,L(dz)=t^{-1}\tau(a_{t^{-1}z},\psi).

Hence, proceeding as above, we obtain

\displaystyle\|\tau(a_{z},\psi(t\,\cdot))\|_{S^{m_{1}},a,b,c}\leq\frac{t^{-1}}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{C}}|\bar{\partial}\tilde{\psi}(z)|\ \|a_{t^{-1}z}\|_{S^{m_{1}},a,b,c}\,L(dz)

\displaystyle\qquad\lesssim t^{-1}\int_{\mathbb{C}}|\bar{\partial}\tilde{\psi}(z)|\left(1+\frac{1+|t^{-1}z|}{|\text{\rm Im}\,t^{-1}z|}\right)^{p}L(dz)\leq t^{-1}\int_{\mathbb{C}}|\bar{\partial}\tilde{\psi}(z)|\left(1+\frac{1+|z|}{|\text{\rm Im}\,z|}\right)^{p}L(dz)

\displaystyle\qquad\lesssim t^{-1}\|\psi\|_{\mathcal{G}^{m^{\prime}},N}.

This concludes the proof.
∎

Corollary 6.12
.

We continue with the setting of Theorem
6.7
and Lemma
6.10
with
m_{1}\leq-m
.
Moreover we assume that for any seminorm

\|\cdot\|_{S^{m_{1}+m},a,b,c}
, there exist
C=C_{m_{1},a,b,c}>0
and
p\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\forall z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}\qquad\|za_{z}\|_{S^{m_{1}+m},a,b,c}\leq C\left(1+\frac{1+|z|}{|\text{\rm Im}\,z|}\right)^{p}.

(1)

For any
m_{2}\in[-1,0]
and any seminorm
\|\cdot\|_{S^{m_{1}+m_{2}m},a,b,c}
,
there exist a constant
C>0
and a number
d_{0}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that we have for any
\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\frac{1}{2},2)
and
t\in(0,1]

\|\tau(a_{z},\psi(t\,\cdot))\|_{S^{m_{1}+m_{2}m},a,b,c}\leq Ct^{m_{2}}\max_{d=0,\ldots,d_{0}}\sup_{\lambda\geq 0}|\psi^{(d)}(\lambda)|,

(2)

For any
m^{\prime}_{2}\geq-m
and any seminorm
\|\cdot\|_{S^{m_{1}+m_{2}^{\prime}},a,b,c}
,
there exist a constant
C>0
and a number
d_{0}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that

\forall\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(-1,1)\qquad\|\tau(a_{z},\psi)\|_{S^{m_{1}+m^{\prime}_{2}},a,b,c}\leq C\max_{d=0,\ldots,d_{0}}\sup_{\lambda\geq 0}|\psi^{(d)}(\lambda)|.

Remark 6.13
.

We observe that the hypotheses on
a_{z}
in Corollary
6.12
are satisfied by

b_{k,z}
.
Indeed, we have

zb_{k,z}=z(z-\sigma_{0})^{-1}d_{k,z}=\left(1+\sigma_{0}(z-\sigma_{0})^{-1}\right)d_{k,z},

and

b_{k,z}
and
d_{k,z}
satisfy the hypotheses on
a_{z}
in Lemma
6.10
by Remark
6.11
.
Therefore, the hypotheses on
a_{z}
in Corollary
6.12
are also satisfied by

p_{z}
and

p_{z}-\sum_{k=0}^{N}\varepsilon^{k}b_{k,z}
.

Proof of Corollary
6.12
.

Part (2) follows directly from Lemma
6.10
with the continuity of the inclusion of symbol classes.
Let us prove Part (1).
Let
\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\frac{1}{2},2)
.
Lemma
6.10
gives the case of
m_{2}=-1
.
For
m_{2}=0
, consider
\psi_{1}(\lambda):=\lambda^{-1}\psi(\lambda)
.
Let
\tilde{\psi}_{1}
be the almost analytic extension for
\psi_{1}
constructed in
Proposition
B.1
.
We observe that
tz\tilde{\psi}_{1}(tz)
is an
almost analytical extension of
\psi(t\lambda)
.
We have

\tau(a_{z},\psi(t\,\cdot))=t\tau(za_{z},\psi_{1}(t\,\cdot)).

The result follows from Lemma
6.10
for
m_{2}=0
, and for any
m_{2}\in(-1,0)
by interpolation.
∎

We can now show Theorem
6.7
.

Proof of Theorem
6.7
.

By the properties of the semiclassical calculus,
it suffices to show the case of
m^{\prime}\in[-1/2,0)
.
Let
\psi\in\mathcal{G}^{m^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with fixed
m^{\prime}\in[-1/2,0)
.
Without loss of generality,
we may assume that
\psi
is real-valued.
Let
(\eta_{j})
be the dyadic decomposition considered in Section
4.8.2
.
We may write for any
\lambda\geq 0

\psi(\lambda)=\psi_{-1}(\lambda)+\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{jm^{\prime}}\psi_{j}(2^{-j}\lambda),

where

\psi_{j}(\mu):=2^{-jm^{\prime}}\psi(2^{j}\mu)\eta_{0}(\mu),\ j\geq 0,\quad\mbox{and}\quad\psi_{-1}(\mu):=\psi(\mu)\eta_{-1}(\mu).

We observe

\psi_{-1}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(-1,1)\quad\mbox{while}\quad\psi_{j}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\frac{1}{2},2),\ j\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\sup_{\lambda\geq 0}|\psi_{j}^{(d)}(\lambda)|\lesssim_{d}\|\psi\|_{\mathcal{G}^{m^{\prime}},d}

for any
d\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

with an implicit constant independent of
j=-1,0,1,2,\ldots
.

Considering the setting of Lemma
6.10
, we can proceed as in the proof of
Theorem
4.32
(2)
replacing the role of the resolvent of
\sigma_{0}
with
a_{z}
.
An application of the Cotlar-Stein Lemma together with Corollary
6.12
(1)
implies

\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{jm^{\prime}}\tau(a_{z},\psi_{j}(2^{-j}\,\cdot))\in S^{mm^{\prime}+m_{1}}(G\times{\widehat{G}}),

with seminorm estimates; note that this requires
m^{\prime}\in[-1/2,0)
.
Using Corollary
6.12
(2) for the first term corresponding to
j=-1
,
we may define

\tilde{\tau}(a_{z},\psi):=\tau(a_{z},\psi_{-1})+\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{jm^{\prime}}\tau(b_{k,z},\psi_{j}(2^{-j}\,\cdot))\in S^{mm^{\prime}+m_{1}}(G\times{\widehat{G}}).

Given a seminorm
\|\cdot\|_{S^{mm^{\prime}+m_{1}}(G\times{\widehat{G}}),a,b,c}
there exist a constant
C^{\prime}
and a seminorm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{G}^{m^{\prime}},N}
both independent of
\psi
such that

\|\tilde{\tau}(a_{z},\psi)\|_{S^{mm^{\prime}+m_{1}}(G\times{\widehat{G}}),a,b,c}\leq C\|\psi\|_{\mathcal{G}^{m^{\prime}},N}.

In fact, the constant
C^{\prime}
is up to a constant given by the constant
C_{k,a^{\prime},b^{\prime},c^{\prime}}
from Lemma
6.10
with
a^{\prime},b^{\prime},c^{\prime}
high enough.
We can apply this to
a_{z}
being
b_{k,z}
,

p_{z}
and

p_{z}-\sum_{k=0}^{N}\varepsilon^{k}b_{k,z}
, see
Remarks
6.11
and

6.13
.
Consequently, the estimate in Lemma
6.8
implies for the latter

\|\tilde{\tau}(p_{z},\psi)-\sum_{k=0}^{N}\varepsilon^{k}\tilde{\tau}(b_{k,z},\psi)\|_{S^{mm^{\prime}-N},a,b,c}=\|\tilde{\tau}(p_{z}-\sum_{k=0}^{N}\varepsilon^{k}b_{k,z},\psi)\|_{S^{mm^{\prime}-N},a,b,c}\lesssim\varepsilon^{N+1}\|\psi\|_{\mathcal{G}^{m^{\prime}},N}.

In other words, the symbol

s_{\psi}:=\tilde{\tau}(p_{z},\psi)\in S^{mm^{\prime}}(G\times{\widehat{G}}),

admits the following semiclassical asymptotics expansion

s_{\psi}\sim_{\varepsilon}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\varepsilon^{k}\tau_{k},\qquad\mbox{where}\quad\tau_{k}:=\tilde{\tau}(b_{k,z},\psi),\ k=0,1,2,\ldots

It remains to show that the family of operators

R:=R(\varepsilon):=\psi(T)-{\rm Op}^{(\varepsilon)}_{G}(s_{\psi}),\quad\varepsilon\in(0,1],

is semiclassically smoothing on the Sobolev scale.
Above,
\psi(T)
is defined by functional analysis and
we have with a sum in the sense of the strong operator topology of
L^{2}(G)
:

\psi(T)=\psi_{-1}(T)+\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{jm^{\prime}}\psi_{j}(2^{-j}T).

We now use the right parametrix
P_{z}
constructed in Lemma
6.8
, to write

(z-T)P_{z}=\text{\rm I}+R_{z},\quad\mbox{so}\quad(z-T)^{-1}=P_{z}-(z-T)^{-1}R_{z}.

With the Helffer–Sjöstrand formula (see (
4.7
)), this leads to decomposing
\psi_{0}(T)
as

\psi_{0}(T)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{C}}\bar{\partial}\tilde{\psi}_{0}(z)\ (z-T)^{-1}L(dz)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{C}}\bar{\partial}\tilde{\psi}_{0}(z)\ P_{z}\,L(dz)+R_{0,\psi},

where

R_{0,\psi}:=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{C}}\bar{\partial}\tilde{\psi}_{0}(z)\ (z-T)^{-1}R_{z}\,L(dz).

We recognise

\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{C}}\bar{\partial}\tilde{\psi}_{0}(z)\ P_{z}\,L(dz)={\rm Op}_{G}^{(\varepsilon)}(\tau(p_{z},\psi_{0})).

More generally, we obtain for any
j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and for
j=-1
:

\displaystyle R_{j,\psi}

\displaystyle:=\psi_{j}(2^{-j}T)-{\rm Op}_{G}^{(\varepsilon)}(\tau(p_{z},\psi_{j}(2^{-j}\cdot)))=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{C}}2^{-j}\bar{\partial}\tilde{\psi}_{j}(2^{-j}z)\ (z-T)^{-1}R_{z}\,L(dz),

\displaystyle R_{-1,\psi}

\displaystyle:=\psi_{-1}(T)-{\rm Op}_{G}^{(\varepsilon)}(\tau(p_{z},\psi_{-1}))=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{C}}\bar{\partial}\tilde{\psi}_{-1}(z)\ (z-T)^{-1}R_{z}\,L(dz).

With a sum in the sense of the strong operator topology of
L^{2}(G)
, we can now write

R=R_{-1,\psi}+\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{jm^{\prime}}R_{j,\psi}.

Let us analyse
R_{0,\psi}
.
The properties of the semiclassical calculus together with
Lemmata
6.8
(3) and
6.9
imply

\|(z-T)^{-1}R_{z}(\varepsilon)\|_{\mathscr{L}(L^{2}_{s,\varepsilon}(G),L^{2}_{s+N+m,\varepsilon}(G))}\lesssim_{N,m}\left(1+\frac{1+|z|}{|\text{\rm Im}\,z|}\right)^{p}\varepsilon^{N+1},

for some
p\in\mathbb{N}
.
This yields

\|R_{0,\psi}\|_{\mathscr{L}(L^{2}_{s,\varepsilon}(G),L^{2}_{s+N+m,\varepsilon}(G))}\lesssim_{N,m}\varepsilon^{N+1}\|\psi\|_{\mathcal{G}^{m^{\prime}},N_{1}},

for some
N_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
.
We have similar results for the
R_{j,\psi}
for
j=-1
and
j=1,2,\ldots
, with the implicit constants in the estimates independent of
j
.
Therefore, we have obtained:

\|R\|_{\mathscr{L}(L^{2}_{s,\varepsilon}(G),L^{2}_{s+N+m,\varepsilon}(G))}\lesssim_{N,m}\varepsilon^{N+1}\|\psi\|_{\mathcal{G}^{m^{\prime}},N_{1}}(1+\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{jm^{\prime}})\lesssim\varepsilon^{N+1}\|\psi\|_{\mathcal{G}^{m^{\prime}},N_{1}}.

This concludes the proof.
∎

In the Abelian case, it is possible to give a very neat formula for the
\tau_{k}
in terms of derivatives of
\psi
.
Although this is not possible in general in the non-commutative case, our proof still shows that the dependence of the
\tau_{k}
is linear and continuous in
\psi
:

Corollary 6.14
.

We continue with the setting of Theorem
6.7
.
Our construction is such that
s_{\psi}
,
\tau_{k}
and
R
depend linearly on
\psi
. Moreover, they satisfy the following estimates uniformly in
\varepsilon\in(0,1]

\|s_{\psi}\|_{S^{mm^{\prime}},a,b,c}\leq C\|\psi\|_{\mathcal{G}^{m^{\prime}},N},\qquad\|\tau_{k}\|_{S^{mm^{\prime}-k},a,b,c}\leq C\|\psi\|_{\mathcal{G}^{m^{\prime}},N},

and on
G

\|R\|_{\mathscr{L}(L^{2}_{s,\varepsilon}(G),L^{2}_{s+N_{1},\varepsilon}(G))}\leq C\varepsilon^{N^{\prime}+1}\|\psi\|_{\mathcal{G}^{m^{\prime}},N},

while on
M
,
R={\rm Op}_{M}^{(\varepsilon)}(r)
with

\|r\|_{S^{-N_{1}},a,b,c}\leq C\varepsilon^{N^{\prime}+1}\|\psi\|_{\mathcal{G}^{m^{\prime}},N}.

Above,
C,N
depend on
N^{\prime},N_{1}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
a,b,c
but not on
\psi,\varepsilon
.

7.
Weyl laws

In this section, we apply the functional calculus developed above to prove Weyl laws of certain semiclassical operators.

7.1.
Results

Considering setting of the functional calculus above,
we will prove Weyl laws under the following hypotheses:

Hypothesis 7.1
.

We assume that for two numbers
a<b
there exists a
\delta_{0}>0
such that on
G

C_{\sigma_{0},a,b,\delta_{0}}:=\int_{G\times{\widehat{G}}}{\rm Tr}|1_{(a-\delta_{0},b+\delta_{0})}(\sigma_{0}(x,\pi))|d\mu(\pi)dx<\infty.

We have a similar hypothesis on
M
, with an integration over
M\times{\widehat{G}}
.

Theorem 7.2
.

We consider Setting
6.1
and Hypotheses
6.2
,
6.3
and
7.1
.
Then all the point of
[a,b]
in the spectrum of
T
are point spectrum.
Denote by
\lambda_{j,\varepsilon}
,
j\in\mathbb{N}
, the eigenvalues of
T
contained in the interval
[a,b]
(counted with multiplicities and in increasing order)
and the corresponding spectral counting function for a fixed
[a,b]\subset\mathbb{R}
by

N(\varepsilon):=|\{\lambda_{j,\varepsilon}\in[a,b]\}|.

We have

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{Q}N(\varepsilon)=\int_{G\times{\widehat{G}}}{\rm Tr}\left(1_{[a,b]}(\sigma_{0}(x,\pi))\right)dxd\mu(\pi).

We have a similar result on
M
with

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{Q}N(\varepsilon)=\int_{M\times{\widehat{G}}}{\rm Tr}\left(1_{[a,b]}(\sigma_{0}(x,\pi))\right)dxd\mu(\pi).

Before discussing the proof of Theorem
7.2
, let us discuss its main application to a sub-Laplacian in divergence form perturbed by a potential:

Corollary 7.3
.

Let
\mathcal{L}_{A}
be a non-negative sub-Laplacian in horizontal divergence form on a stratified group
G
as in Section
4.9
. We assume that it satisfies the hypothesis of uniform ellipticity of Lemma
4.39
.
Let
V\in C^{\infty}(G)
be a non-negative function
such that all its left-invariant derivatives are bounded. Moreover, suppose that there exists
a<b
and
\delta>0
such that
V^{-1}((a-\delta,b+\delta))
is compact.
Then the operators

T(\varepsilon):=\varepsilon^{2}\mathcal{L}_{A}+V,\quad\varepsilon\in(0,1],

satisfy the Weyl law

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{Q}{\rm Tr}[1_{[a,b]}(T(\varepsilon))]=\int_{G\times{\widehat{G}}}{\rm Tr}\left(1_{[a,b]}(\tau_{0}(x,\pi))\right)dxd\mu(\pi),

where
\tau_{0}=\sigma_{0}+V
, that is,

\tau_{0}(x,\pi)=\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq n_{1}}a_{i,j}(x)\,\pi(X_{i})\pi(X_{j})+V(x).

We have a similar result on the nilmanifold
M
.

Proof.

With the notation of Section
4.9
, we have

T(\varepsilon)={\rm Op}_{G}^{(\varepsilon)}(\tau(\varepsilon)),\quad\mbox{where}\quad\tau(\varepsilon)=\tau_{0}+\varepsilon\tau_{1},

with principal and subprincipal symbols

\tau_{0}(x,\pi)=\sigma_{0}(x,\pi)+V(x)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\tau_{1}(x,\pi)=\sigma_{1}(x,\pi).

As
V\geq 0
and
V^{-1}((a-\delta,b+\delta))
is compact,
V^{-1}([0,b+\delta))
is compact and contained
V^{-1}((a-\delta,b+\delta))
.
The uniform ellipticity implies:

0\leq\sigma_{0}(x,\pi)\leq C\pi(\mathcal{L}_{\text{\rm I}}),

so that

0\leq 1_{(a-\delta_{0},b+\delta_{0})}(\tau_{0}(x,\pi))\leq 1_{[0,b+\delta)}(V(x))\ |\psi|^{2}(C\pi(\mathcal{L}_{\text{\rm I}})),

where
\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
is valued in
[0,1]
and satisfies
\psi=1
on
(-\infty,b+\delta)
.
This implies that the quantity

\displaystyle\int_{G\times{\widehat{G}}}{\rm Tr}|1_{(a-\delta_{0},b+\delta_{0})}(\tau_{0}(x,\pi))|d\mu(\pi)dx

\displaystyle\leq\int_{G}1_{[0,b+\delta)}(V(x))dx\int_{{\widehat{G}}}{\rm Tr}|\psi|^{2}(\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\text{\rm I}})d\mu

\displaystyle\quad=|V^{-1}([0,b+\delta))|\|\psi(\mathcal{L}_{\text{\rm I}})\delta_{0}\|_{L^{2}(G))}^{2}<\infty,

is finite. In other words, Hypothesis
7.1
is satisfied.
We conclude with an application of
Theorem
7.2
.
∎

Let us contrast Corollary
7.3
with another application of Theorem
7.2
valid only on
M
:

Corollary 7.4
.

Let
\mathcal{L}_{A}
be a non-negative sub-Laplacian in horizontal divergence form on a nilmanifold
M
as in Section
4.9
. We assume that it satisfies the hypothesis of uniform ellipticity of Corollary
4.40
.
Let
V\in C^{\infty}(M)
be a non-negative function.
Then the operator
\mathcal{L}_{A}+V
is essentially self-adjoint on
C^{\infty}(M)\subset L^{2}(M)
, has purely discrete spectrum with Weyl laws:

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\lambda^{-Q/2}{\rm Tr}[1_{[0,\lambda]}(\mathcal{L}_{A}+V)]=\int_{M\times{\widehat{G}}}{\rm Tr}\left(1_{[0,1]}(\sigma_{0}(x,\pi))\right)dxd\mu(\pi).

Proof.

We observe

{\rm Tr}[1_{[0,\lambda]}(\mathcal{L}_{A}+V)]={\rm Tr}[1_{[0,1]}(T(\varepsilon))],\quad\mbox{where}\ \varepsilon=\lambda^{-1/2},

and

T(\varepsilon)=\varepsilon^{2}(\mathcal{L}_{A}+V)={\rm Op}^{(\varepsilon)}_{M}(\tau),

with

\tau=\tau_{0}+\varepsilon\tau_{1}+\varepsilon^{2}\tau_{2},\qquad\tau_{0}=\sigma_{0},\ \tau_{1}=\sigma_{1},\ \tau_{2}=V.

As
\sigma_{0}\leq C\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\text{\rm I}}
, we have

0\leq 1_{[0,\lambda]}(\tau_{0}(\dot{x},\pi))\leq|\psi|^{2}(C\pi(\varepsilon^{2}\mathcal{L}_{\text{\rm I}})),

where
\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
is valued in
[0,1]
and satisfies
\psi=1
on
(-\infty,b+\delta)
.
This readily implies Hypothesis
7.1
and we conclude with an application of
Theorem
7.2
.
∎

We can obtain similar results to Corollaries
7.3

and
7.4
by applying Theorem
7.2
to the operators described in Example
6.6
.

7.2.
Proof of Theorem
7.2

We will start the proof of Theorem
7.2
with properties that are of interest of their own.

Proposition 7.5
.

We consider Setting
6.1
and Hypotheses
6.2
,
6.3
and
7.1
on
G
.
For any a compact interval
I\subset(a-\delta_{0},b+\delta_{0})
,
there exists
\varepsilon_{0}\in(0,1]
and
C>0

such that

(7.1)

\forall\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(I),\ \forall\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]\qquad{\rm Tr}|\psi(T)|\leq C\sup_{\mathbb{R}}|\psi|\ \varepsilon^{-Q}.

Moreover, the norm

\|\psi(\sigma_{0})\|_{L^{2}(G\times{\widehat{G}})}^{2}=\int_{G\times{\widehat{G}}}\|\psi(\sigma_{0}(x,\pi))\|_{HS(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})}^{2}dxd\mu(\pi)\leq C_{\sigma_{0},a,b,\delta_{0}}\sup_{\mathbb{R}}|\psi|^{2},\quad

is finite, and we have

\|\psi(T)\|_{HS(L^{2}(G))}^{2}=\varepsilon^{-Q}\int_{G\times{\widehat{G}}}\|\psi(\sigma_{0}(x,\pi))\|_{HS(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})}^{2}dxd\mu(\pi)+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{1-Q}).

A similar result holds on
M
.

This will require the following lemma:

Lemma 7.6
.

We consider Setting
6.1
and Hypotheses
6.2
,
6.3
and
7.1
on
G
. We have
for any
f\in C_{c}(a-\delta_{0},b+\delta_{0})
:

\forall\varepsilon\in(0,1]\qquad\|{\rm Op}^{(\varepsilon)}_{G}(f(\sigma_{0}))\|^{2}_{HS(L^{2}(G))}=\varepsilon^{-Q}\|f(\sigma_{0})\|_{L^{2}(G\times{\widehat{G}})}^{2},

and

\|f(\sigma_{0})\|_{L^{2}(G\times{\widehat{G}})}\leq\sup_{\mathbb{R}}|f|\,C_{\sigma_{0},a,b,\delta_{0}}.

We have a similar result on
M
.

Proof of Lemma
7.6
.

Let
f\in C_{c}(a-\delta_{0},b+\delta_{0})
.
By the Plancherel formula (see also the proof of Corollary
4.17
(2)),
we have

\displaystyle\|{\rm Op}^{(\varepsilon)}_{G}(f(\sigma_{0}))\|^{2}_{HS(L^{2}(G))}

\displaystyle=\int_{G\times{\widehat{G}}}\|f(\sigma_{0})(x,\varepsilon\cdot\pi)\|_{HS(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})}^{2}dxd\mu(\pi)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{-Q}\int_{G\times{\widehat{G}}}\|f(\sigma_{0})(x,\pi^{\prime})\|_{HS(\mathcal{H}_{\pi^{\prime}})}^{2}dxd\mu(\pi^{\prime})

with the change of variable
\pi^{\prime}=\varepsilon\cdot\pi
, see (
3.1
). This gives the equality.
By functional calculus, we have

\|f(\sigma_{0})(x,\pi)\|_{HS(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})}^{2}\leq\sup_{\mathbb{R}}|f|^{2}\,{\rm Tr}|1_{(a-\delta_{0},b+\delta_{0})}(\sigma(x,\pi))|,

and this implies the inequality.
∎

We can now prove Proposition
7.5
.

Proof of Proposition
7.5
.

We fix an interval
I\subset(a-\delta_{0},b+\delta_{0})
.and a function
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(a-\delta,b+\delta)
valued in
[0,1]
and such that
f=1
on
I
.
The operator defined via

R_{f}:=R_{f}(\varepsilon):=\varepsilon^{-1}\left(f^{2}(T)-{\rm Op}^{(\varepsilon)}_{G}(f(\sigma_{0}))\left({\rm Op}^{(\varepsilon)}_{G}(f(\sigma_{0}))\right)^{*}\right),

satisfies for any
\varepsilon\in(0,1]

\|R_{f}\|_{\mathscr{L}(L^{2}(G))}\leq\|R_{f}\|_{\mathscr{L}(L^{2}(G),L^{2}_{1,\varepsilon}(G))}\lesssim\varepsilon,

by Theorem
6.7
and the properties of the semiclassical pseudo-differential calculus.
Hence there exists
\varepsilon_{0}\in(0,1]
sufficiently small such that the operator
\text{\rm I}-\varepsilon R_{f}(\varepsilon))
has an inverse in
\mathscr{L}(L^{2}(G))
for every
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]
with

(7.2)

\|(\text{\rm I}-\varepsilon R_{f}))^{-1}\|_{\mathscr{L}(L^{2}(G))}\leq\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\|\varepsilon R_{f}\|_{\mathscr{L}(L^{2}(G))}^{j}\lesssim 1.

Let
\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(I)
.
Since
\psi=\psi f^{2}
, we have by functional calculus

A=AB,\quad\mbox{with}\ A:=\psi(T),\ B:=f^{2}(T),

while by Theorem
6.7
and the properties of the semiclassical function calculus,

B=B_{0}+\varepsilon R_{f},\quad\mbox{with}\ B_{0}:={\rm Op}^{(\varepsilon)}_{G}(f(\sigma_{0}))\,({\rm Op}^{(\varepsilon)}_{G}(f(\sigma_{0})))^{*}.

Therefore, we have for any
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
,

A=A(B_{0}+\varepsilon R_{f}),\quad\mbox{or equivalently}\ A(\text{\rm I}-\varepsilon R_{f})=AB_{0},

yielding for
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]
,

A=AB_{0}(\text{\rm I}-\varepsilon R_{f})^{-1},

hence,

{\rm Tr}|A|\leq\|A\|_{\mathscr{L}(L^{2}(G))}\|(\text{\rm I}-\varepsilon R_{f})^{-1}\|_{\mathscr{L}(L^{2}(G))}{\rm Tr}|B_{0}|\lesssim\|\psi\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\|{\rm Op}^{(\varepsilon)}_{G}(f(\sigma_{0}))\|^{2}_{HS(L^{2}(G))},

having used the (
7.2
) and functional analysis for the
\mathscr{L}(L^{2}(G))
-norm.
Using Lemma
7.6
on the Hilbert-Schmidt norm,
we obtain (
7.1
).

Proceeding as above for
f=\psi
and
\varepsilon=1
, we obtain the formula for the norm
\|\psi(\sigma_{0})\|_{L^{2}(G\times{\widehat{G}})}
and its estimate.
By Theorem
6.7
and the properties of the semiclassical function calculus, we have

(7.3)

|\psi|^{2}(T)={\rm Op}^{(\varepsilon)}_{G}(|\psi|^{2}(\sigma_{0}))+\varepsilon R_{1},

and similarly for
|f|^{2}(T)
.
Since
|f|^{2}(T)
is self-adjoint,
we have

\displaystyle\|\psi(T)\|_{HS(L^{2}(G))}^{2}

\displaystyle={\rm Tr}(|\psi|^{2}(T))={\rm Tr}(|\psi|^{2}(T)(|f|^{2}(T))^{*})

\displaystyle={\rm Tr}\left(|\psi|^{2}(T)\left({\rm Op}^{(\varepsilon)}_{G}(|f|^{2}(\sigma_{0}))\right)^{*}\right)+O(\varepsilon^{1-Q})

\displaystyle={\rm Tr}\left({\rm Op}^{(\varepsilon)}_{G}(|\psi|^{2}(\sigma_{0}))\left({\rm Op}^{(\varepsilon)}_{G}(|f|^{2}(\sigma_{0}))\right)^{*}\right)+O(\varepsilon^{1-Q}),

having used
(
7.3
) for
f
and
\psi
together with (
7.1
).
Bilinearising the first equality in (
7.6
) implies:

\displaystyle{\rm Tr}\left({\rm Op}^{(\varepsilon)}_{G}(|\psi|^{2}(\sigma_{0}))\left({\rm Op}^{(\varepsilon)}_{G}(|f|^{2}(\sigma_{0}))\right)^{*}\right)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{-Q}\int_{G\times{\widehat{G}}}{\rm Tr}\left(|\psi|^{2}(\sigma_{0}))\left(|f|^{2}(\sigma_{0})\right)^{*}\right)dxd\mu

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{-Q}\int_{G\times{\widehat{G}}}\|\psi(\sigma_{0}(x,\pi))\|_{HS(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})}^{2}dxd\mu(\pi).

The statement follows.
∎

We can now conclude the proof of Theorem
7.2
.

Proof of Theorem
7.2
.

Let
\underline{\chi}
and
\overline{\chi}
be two smooth functions on
\mathbb{R}
, valued in
[0,1]
, satisfying

\overline{\chi}1_{[a,b]}=1_{[a,b]},\quad\mbox{and}\quad\underline{\chi}1_{[a,b]}=\underline{\chi}.

Then

{\rm Tr}\left(|\underline{\chi}|^{2}(T)\right)\leq N(\varepsilon)={\rm Tr}\left(1_{[a,b]}(T)\right)\leq{\rm Tr}\left(|\overline{\chi}|^{2}(T)\right).

By Proposition
7.5
, this implies

\displaystyle\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{-Q}N(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{G\times{\widehat{G}}}\|\overline{\chi}(\sigma_{0}(x,\pi))\|_{HS(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})}^{2}dxd\mu(\pi),

\displaystyle\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{-Q}N(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\geq\int_{G\times{\widehat{G}}}\|\underline{\chi}(\sigma_{0}(x,\pi))\|_{HS(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})}^{2}dxd\mu(\pi).

This is true for any
\underline{\chi}
,
\overline{\chi}
as above. By considering sequences of such functions converging point-wise to
1_{[a,b]}
, the result follows by Lebesgue dominated convergence.
∎

Appendix A
Proof of the semiclassical composition and adjoint

In this section, we give a detailed proof of Theorem
5.1
.
We start with some tools required in the proof.

A.1.
Adapted Taylor estimates and Leibniz properties

A.1.1.
Adapted Taylor estimates

Our analysis will require Taylor estimates adapted to graded groups and due to Folland and Stein
[
FS82b
]
, see also Theorem 3.1.51 in
[
FR16
]
.

Theorem A.1
.

Let
G
be a graded Lie group, with adapted basis
(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})
for its Lie algebra.
We fix a quasinorm
|\cdot|
on
G
.

•

Mean value theorem
. There exists
C_{0}>0
and
\eta>1
such that for any
f\in C^{1}(G)
we have

\forall x,y\in G,\qquad|f(xy)-f(x)|\leq C_{0}\sum_{j=1}^{n}|y|^{\upsilon_{j}}\sup_{|z|\leq\eta|y|}|(X_{j}f)(xz)|.

•

Taylor estimate
.
More generally,
with the constant
\eta
of point (1),
for any
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
there exists
C_{N}>0

such that for any
f\in C^{\lceil N\rfloor}(G)
we have

\forall x,y\in G,\qquad|f(xy)-\mathbb{P}_{G,f,x,N}(y)|\leq C_{N}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\alpha|\leq\lceil N\rfloor+1\\
[\alpha]>N\end{subarray}}|y|^{[\alpha]}\sup_{|z|\leq\eta^{\lceil N\rfloor+1}|y|}|(\mathbb{V}^{\alpha}f)(xz)|.

Above,
\lceil N\rfloor
denotes
\max\{|\alpha|:\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}
with
[\alpha]\leq N\}

and

\mathbb{P}_{G,f,x,N}
denotes the Taylor polynomial of
f
at
x
of order
N
for the graded group
G
, i.e. the unique linear combination of monomials of homogeneous degree
\leq N
satisfying

X^{\beta}\mathbb{P}_{G,f,x,N}(0)=X^{\beta}f(x)
for any
\beta\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}
with
[\beta]\leq N
.

A.1.2.
Leibniz properties

.
The Leibnitz properties of vector fields readily imply for the product of symbols:

X^{\beta}(\tau_{1}\tau_{2})=\sum_{[\beta_{1}]+[\beta_{2}]=\beta}c^{\prime}_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha}X^{\beta_{1}}\tau_{1}\ X^{\beta_{2}}\tau_{2},

as well as for their
\diamond
-products:

X^{\beta}(\sigma_{1}\diamond_{\varepsilon}\sigma_{2})=\sum_{[\beta_{1}]+[\beta_{2}]=\beta}c^{\prime}_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha}\int_{G}\kappa_{X^{\beta_{1}}\sigma_{1},x_{1}}(z)\,\pi(z)^{*}X^{\beta_{2}}_{x_{2}=x}\sigma_{2}(x_{2}\,\varepsilon\cdot z^{-1},\pi)\,dz

A similar property holds for the difference operators.
Indeed, recall that the
(q_{\alpha})
is a homogeneous basis of polynomials, and therefore it satisfies
[
FR16
, Section 3.1.4]

q_{\alpha}(xy)=\sum_{[\alpha_{1}]+[\alpha_{2}]=[\alpha]}c_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha}q_{\alpha_{1}}(y)q_{\alpha_{2}}(x).

This implies the Leibniz properties for
\Delta^{\alpha}

for the product of symbols

\Delta^{\alpha}(\tau_{1}\tau_{2})=\sum_{[\alpha_{1}]+[\alpha_{2}]=[\alpha]}c_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha}\Delta^{\alpha_{1}}\tau_{1}\Delta^{\alpha_{2}}\tau_{2},

and also for the
\diamond
-product resulting in
\sigma
:

\Delta^{\alpha}(\sigma_{1}\diamond_{\varepsilon}\sigma_{2})=\sum_{[\alpha_{1}]+[\alpha_{2}]=[\alpha]}c_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha}\Delta^{\alpha_{1}}\sigma_{1}\diamond_{\varepsilon}\Delta^{\alpha_{2}}\sigma_{2}.

A.2.
Proof for the composition

Here, we prove Theorem
5.1
(1).
Let
\sigma_{1}\in S^{m_{1}}(G\times{\widehat{G}})

and
\sigma_{2}\in S^{m_{1}}(G\times{\widehat{G}})
.
By Theorem
4.11
,

{\rm Op}_{G}^{(\varepsilon)}(\sigma_{1}){\rm Op}_{G}^{(\varepsilon)}(\sigma_{2})={\rm Op}_{G}^{(\varepsilon)}(\sigma)\in\Psi^{m_{1}+m_{2}}(G\times{\widehat{G}})

where

\sigma:=\sigma_{1}\diamond_{\varepsilon}\sigma_{2}:=(\sigma_{1}(\cdot,\varepsilon\,\cdot)\diamond\sigma_{2}(\cdot,\varepsilon\,\cdot)))(\cdot,\varepsilon^{-1}\,\cdot),

depends on
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
.
By (
4.2
),
the convolution kernel of
\sigma
is

\kappa_{x}(y)=\varepsilon^{Q}\int_{G}\kappa^{(\varepsilon)}_{\sigma_{2},xz^{-1}}((\varepsilon\cdot y)z^{-1})\ \kappa^{(\varepsilon)}_{1,x}(z)dz=\int_{G}\kappa_{\sigma_{2},x(\varepsilon\cdot z)^{-1}}(yz^{-1})\ \kappa_{\sigma_{1},x}(z)dz.

Therefore

\sigma(x,\pi)=\int_{G}\kappa_{x}(y)\pi(y)^{*}dy=\int_{G}\kappa_{\sigma_{1},x}(z)\,\pi(z)^{*}\sigma_{2}(x\,\varepsilon\cdot z^{-1},\pi)\,dz.

By the Taylor estimates due to Folland
and Stein (see Theorem
A.1
),

\sigma_{2}(x\,\varepsilon\cdot z^{-1},\pi)=\sum_{[\alpha]\leq N}q_{\alpha}((\varepsilon\cdot z)^{-1})X^{\alpha}_{x}\sigma_{2}(x,\pi)+R_{x,N}^{\sigma_{2}(\cdot,\pi)}(\varepsilon\cdot z^{-1}),

with remainder estimate

\displaystyle\|(\text{\rm I}+\pi(\mathcal{R}))^{-\frac{m_{2}+\gamma}{\nu}}R_{x,N}^{\sigma_{2}(\cdot,\pi)}(\varepsilon\cdot z^{-1})(\text{\rm I}+\pi(\mathcal{R}))^{\frac{\gamma}{\nu}}\|_{\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})}

\displaystyle\quad\leq C_{N}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\alpha|\leq\lceil N\rfloor\\
[\alpha]>N\end{subarray}}(\varepsilon|z|)^{[\alpha]}\sup_{z^{\prime}\in G}\|(\text{\rm I}+\pi(\mathcal{R}))^{-\frac{m_{2}+\gamma}{\nu}}\sigma_{2}(z^{\prime},\pi)(\text{\rm I}+\pi(\mathcal{R}))^{\frac{\gamma}{\nu}}\|_{\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})}

\displaystyle\quad\leq C_{N,\sigma_{2}}\varepsilon^{N+1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\alpha|\leq\lceil N\rfloor\\
[\alpha]>N\end{subarray}}|z|^{[\alpha]}.

We are led to study

\displaystyle\int_{G}\kappa_{\sigma_{1},x}(z)\,\pi(z)^{*}R_{x,N}^{\sigma_{2}(\cdot,\pi)}(\varepsilon\cdot z^{-1})\,dz

\displaystyle\qquad=\sigma(x,\pi)-\sum_{[\alpha]\leq N}\int_{G}\kappa_{\sigma_{1},x}(z)\,\pi(z)^{*}q_{\alpha}((\varepsilon\cdot z)^{-1})X^{\alpha}_{x}\sigma_{2}(x,\pi)\,dz

\displaystyle\qquad=\sigma(x,\pi)-\sum_{[\alpha]\leq N}\varepsilon^{[\alpha]}\Delta^{\alpha}\sigma(x,\pi)X^{\alpha}_{x}\sigma_{2}(x,\pi).

The
\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})
-norm of this expression for
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
and
m_{2}\leq 0
is estimated by

\|\sigma(x,\pi)-\sum_{[\alpha]\leq N}\varepsilon^{[\alpha]}\Delta^{\alpha}\sigma_{1}(x,\pi)X^{\alpha}_{x}\sigma_{2}(x,\pi)\|_{\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})}\leq C_{N,\sigma_{2}}\varepsilon^{N+1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\alpha|\leq\lceil N\rfloor\\
[\alpha]>N\end{subarray}}\int_{G}|z|^{[\alpha]}|\kappa_{\sigma_{1},x}(z)|dz.

If
N
is large enough (more precisely,
N
such that

m_{1}-N<-Q
), then the integrals on the right-hand side are finite by the kernel estimates (see Theorem
4.15
).
More generally, we have:

\displaystyle\pi(X)^{\alpha_{0}}\left(\sigma(x,\pi)-\sum_{[\alpha]\leq N}\varepsilon^{[\alpha]}\Delta^{\alpha}\sigma(x,\pi)X^{\alpha}_{x}\sigma_{2}(x,\pi)\right)

\displaystyle\qquad=\int_{G}\kappa_{\sigma_{1},x}(z)\,(\widetilde{X}^{\alpha_{0}}_{z}\pi(z))^{*}R_{x,N}^{\sigma_{2}(\cdot,\pi)}(\varepsilon\cdot z^{-1})\,dz

\displaystyle\qquad=(-1)^{|\alpha_{0}|}\int_{G}\pi(z)^{*}\widetilde{X}^{\alpha_{0}}_{z}\left(\kappa_{\sigma_{1},x}(z)\,R_{x,N}^{\sigma_{2}(\cdot,\pi)}(\varepsilon\cdot z^{-1})\right)dz

which, by the Leibniz property of vector fields, is a linear combination over
[\alpha_{0,1}]+[\alpha_{0,2}]=[\alpha_{0}]
of

\displaystyle\int_{G}(\widetilde{X}^{\alpha_{0,1}}\kappa_{\sigma_{1},x})(z)\,\pi(z)^{*}\varepsilon^{[\alpha_{0,2}]}(X^{\alpha_{0,2}}R_{x,N}^{\sigma_{2}(\cdot,\pi)})(\varepsilon\cdot z^{-1})\,dz

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{G}\kappa_{\widehat{X}^{\alpha_{0,1}}\sigma_{1},x}(z)\,\pi(z)^{*}\varepsilon^{[\alpha_{0,2}]}R_{x,N-[\alpha_{0,2}]}^{X^{\alpha_{0,2}}\sigma_{2}(\cdot,\pi)})(\varepsilon\cdot z^{-1})\,dz,

when
N>[\alpha_{0}]
.
Proceeding as above, we obtain:

\displaystyle\Big\|\pi(X)^{\alpha_{0}}\left(\sigma(x,\pi)-\sum_{[\alpha]\leq N}\varepsilon^{[\alpha]}\Delta^{\alpha}\sigma(x,\pi)X^{\alpha}_{x}\sigma_{2}(x,\pi)\right)\Big\|_{\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})}

\displaystyle\quad\leq C_{\alpha_{0}}\sum_{[\alpha_{0,1}]+[\alpha_{0,2}]=[\alpha_{0}]}\int_{G}|\kappa_{\widehat{X}^{\alpha_{0,1}}\sigma_{1},x}(z)|\|R_{x,N-[\alpha_{0,2}]}^{X^{\alpha_{0,2}}\sigma_{2}(\cdot,\pi)})(\varepsilon\cdot z^{-1},\pi)\|_{\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})}\,dz

\displaystyle\quad\leq C_{N,\sigma_{2},\alpha_{0}}\sum_{[\alpha_{0,1}]+[\alpha_{0,2}]=[\alpha_{0}]}\varepsilon^{N+1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\alpha|\leq\lceil N\rfloor\\
[\alpha]>N\end{subarray}}\int_{G}|z|^{[\alpha]}|\kappa_{\widehat{X}^{\alpha_{0,1}}\sigma_{1},x}(z)|dz.

Again,
if
m_{2}\leq 0
and
N
is large enough (this time, with
N
such that

m_{1}+[\alpha_{0}]-N<-Q
), then the integrals on the right-hand side are finite.

If
m_{2}>0
, we proceed as follows.
We consider a positive Rockland operator of homogeneous degree
\nu
of degree high enough and symmetric in the sense that
\mathcal{R}(f(x^{-1}))=\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}f(z^{-1})
; for instance, we take
\mathcal{R}
as in Example
3.1
(3).
We then introduce
\text{\rm I}=(\text{\rm I}+\widehat{\mathcal{R}})(\text{\rm I}+\widehat{\mathcal{R}})^{-1}
in the following way:

\displaystyle\sigma(x,\pi)-\sum_{[\alpha]\leq N}\varepsilon^{[\alpha]}\Delta^{\alpha}\sigma(x,\pi)X^{\alpha}_{x}\sigma_{2}(x,\pi)

\displaystyle\qquad=\int_{G}\kappa_{\sigma_{1},x}(z)\,\pi(z)^{*}(\text{\rm I}+\widehat{\mathcal{R}})(\text{\rm I}+\widehat{\mathcal{R}})^{-1}R_{x,N}^{\sigma_{2}(\cdot,\pi)}(\varepsilon\cdot z^{-1},\pi)\,dz

\displaystyle\qquad=\int_{G}\kappa_{\sigma_{1},x}(z)\,\pi(z)^{*}R_{x,N}^{(\text{\rm I}+\widehat{\mathcal{R}})^{-1}\sigma_{2}(\cdot,\pi)}(\varepsilon\cdot z^{-1},\pi)\,dz

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\int_{G}\kappa_{\sigma_{1},x}(z)\,(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{z}\pi(z)^{*})R_{x,N}^{(\text{\rm I}+\widehat{\mathcal{R}})^{-1}\sigma_{2}(\cdot,\pi)}(\varepsilon\cdot z^{-1},\pi)\,dz.

For the first integral on the right hand side, we proceed as above, while for the second an integration by part yields a linear combination over
[\alpha^{\prime}_{1}]+[\alpha_{2}^{\prime}]=\nu
of

\displaystyle\int_{G}(\widetilde{X}^{\alpha^{\prime}_{1}}\kappa_{\sigma_{1},x})(z)\,(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{z}\pi(z)^{*})\widetilde{X}^{\alpha^{\prime}_{2}}_{z^{\prime}=\varepsilon\cdot z^{-1}}R_{x,N}^{(\text{\rm I}+\widehat{\mathcal{R}})^{-1}\sigma_{2}(\cdot,\pi)}(z^{\prime},\pi)\,dz

\displaystyle\qquad=\int_{G}(\kappa_{\widehat{X}^{\alpha^{\prime}_{1}}\sigma_{1},x})(z)\,\pi(z)^{*}{z^{\prime}=\varepsilon\cdot z^{-1}}R_{0,N-[\alpha^{\prime}_{2}]}^{(\text{\rm I}+\widehat{\mathcal{R}})^{-1}X^{\alpha^{\prime}_{2}}_{x}\sigma_{2}(x\cdot,\pi)}(z^{\prime},\pi)\,dz.

Again, for
N
large enough, we can proceed as above.

We have obtained that for any
m_{1},m_{2}
,
\alpha_{0}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}
, there exists
N_{0}
such that for any
N\geq N_{0}
, there exists
C>0
satisfying

\sup_{x\in G,\pi\in{\widehat{G}}}\|\pi(X)^{\alpha_{0}}(\sigma(x,\pi)-\sum_{[\alpha]\leq N}\varepsilon^{[\alpha]}\Delta^{\alpha}\sigma(x,\pi)X^{\alpha}_{x}\sigma_{2}(x,\pi))\|_{\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})}\leq C\varepsilon^{N+1}.

This implies the desired estimate for the norm
\|\cdot\|_{S^{m_{1}+m_{2}},0,0,0}
.
The estimates for the semi-norms
\|\cdot\|_{S^{m},a,b,0}
is then a consequence of the Leibniz properties presented in Section
A.1.2
.This concludes the proof of Theorem
5.1
(1).

A.3.
Proof for the adjoint

Here, we prove Theorem
5.1
(2).
Let
\sigma\in S^{m}(G\times{\widehat{G}})
. Then

T^{(\varepsilon)}:=({\rm Op}_{G}^{(\varepsilon)}(\sigma))^{*}\in\Psi^{m}(G\times{\widehat{G}})
by Theorem
4.11
, and

T^{(\varepsilon)}={\rm Op}^{(\varepsilon)}_{G}(\sigma)\quad\mbox{where}\quad\sigma:=\sigma^{(\varepsilon,*)}:=(\sigma(\cdot,\varepsilon\,\cdot))^{(*)}(\cdot,\varepsilon^{-1}\,\cdot),

with
\sigma
dependent on
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
.
By (
4.2
),
the convolution kernel of
\sigma
is

\kappa_{x}(y)=\varepsilon^{Q}\bar{\kappa}_{\sigma(\cdot,\varepsilon\,\cdot),x\varepsilon\cdot y^{-1}}(\varepsilon\cdot y^{-1})=\bar{\kappa}_{\sigma,x\varepsilon\cdot y^{-1}}(y^{-1}).

By the Taylor estimates due to Folland and Stein (see Theorem
A.1
,

\kappa_{\sigma,x\varepsilon\cdot y^{-1}}(w)=\sum_{[\alpha]\leq N}q_{\alpha}(\varepsilon\cdot y^{-1})X^{\alpha}_{x}\kappa_{\sigma,x}(w)+R_{x,N}^{\kappa_{\sigma,\cdot}(w)}(\varepsilon\cdot y^{-1}),

with remainder estimate

\displaystyle|R_{x,N}^{\kappa_{\sigma,\cdot}(w)}(\varepsilon\cdot y^{-1})|

\displaystyle\leq C_{N}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\alpha|\leq\lceil N\rfloor\\
[\alpha]>N\end{subarray}}\varepsilon^{[\alpha]}|y|^{[\alpha]}\sup_{|y^{\prime}|\leq\eta^{\lceil N\rfloor+1}\varepsilon|y|}|X^{\alpha}_{x^{\prime}=xy^{\prime}}\kappa_{\sigma,x^{\prime}}(w)|.

By the kernel estimates (see Theorem
A.1
), this implies

\sup_{\varepsilon^{-(N+1)}}|R_{x,N}^{\kappa_{\sigma,\cdot}(w)}(\varepsilon\cdot y^{-1})|\leq C_{\sigma,N_{1}}\varepsilon^{N_{1}}(1+|y|)^{-N_{1}}

for any
N_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
if
m_{1}-N<-Q
.
Hence, we have

\displaystyle\|\sigma^{(\varepsilon,*)}-\sum_{[\alpha]\leq N}\Delta^{\alpha}\sigma^{*}\|_{\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})}

\displaystyle=\|\int_{G}R_{x,N}^{\kappa_{\sigma,\cdot}(w)}(\varepsilon\cdot y^{-1})\pi(y)^{*}dy\|_{\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{G}\|R_{x,N}^{\kappa_{\sigma,\cdot}(w)}(\varepsilon\cdot y^{-1})\|_{\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})}dy

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon^{N_{1}}\int_{G}C_{\sigma,N_{1}}(1+|y|)^{-N_{1}}dy,

is finite if
N_{1}>Q
.
This implies the desired estimate for the norm
\|\cdot\|_{S^{m},0,0,0}
.
The estimates for the semi-norms
\|\cdot\|_{S^{m},a,b,0}
is then a consequence of the Leibniz properties presented in Section
A.1.2
.This concludes the proof of Theorem
5.1
(2).

Appendix B
Proof of Lemma
4.36

This Appendix is devoted to proving Lemma
4.36
.
By density of
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
in
\mathcal{G}^{m^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R})
,
Lemma
4.36
is a consequence of
Lemma
4.35

and the following statement:

Proposition B.1
.

Let
\psi\in\mathcal{G}^{m^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R})
with
m^{\prime}<-1
. Then we can construct an almost analytic extension
\tilde{\psi}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{C})
to
\psi
such that we have for all
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

\int_{\mathbb{C}}\big|\bar{\partial}\tilde{\psi}(z)\big|\left(\frac{1+|z|}{|\text{\rm Im}\,z|}\right)^{N}L(dz)\leq C_{N}\|\psi\|_{\mathcal{G}^{m^{\prime}},N+3},

and for any
N,p\in\mathbb{N}
,

|\partial_{y}^{p}\tilde{\psi}(z)|\leq C_{N,p}\frac{|\text{\rm Im}\,z|^{N}}{(1+|z|)^{p-m^{\prime}}}\|\psi\|_{\mathcal{G}^{m^{\prime}},N+p+2}.

Above the constants
C_{N},C_{N,p}>0
depend on
N
and on
N,p
respectively
(and on the construction and on
m^{\prime}
), but not on
\psi
.
Moreover, we have

\forall k\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\qquad\partial_{x}^{k}\tilde{\psi}|_{\mathbb{R}}=(-i\partial_{y})^{k}\tilde{\psi}|_{\mathbb{R}}=\partial_{x}^{k}\psi,

and

{{\rm supp}}(\tilde{\psi})\subset\{x+iy\in\mathbb{C}\,|\,{\rm dist}(x,{{\rm supp}}(\psi))\leq 1\text{ and }|y|\leq 10(1+|x|)\}.

The proof Lemma
4.36
will be complete once we show Proposition
B.1
.
The proof and construction of the latter is standard,
although the particular estimates for the derivatives in
y
may not always be given explicitely; so,
we include them here.
It is based on the ideas of Jensen and Nakamura in
[
JN94
]
.
The first author would like to take this opportunity to correct a statement that has appeared in her paper
[
Fis19
, Section 3.1.1]
.

The proof of Proposition
B.1
will rely on the following auxiliary results.

Lemma B.2
.

We fix a function
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}:[0,1])
such that
\chi=1
on
[-2,2]
and
\chi=0
outside
[-4,4]
.
Let
\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(-2,2)
.
We set

\tilde{\psi}(z):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{2\pi iz\xi}\chi(y\xi)\widehat{\psi}(\xi)d\xi,\quad z=x+iy.

This defines a smooth function
\tilde{\psi}:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}
. It
is an almost analytic extension of
\psi

that satisfies

\partial_{x}^{k}\tilde{\psi}|_{\mathbb{R}}=(-i\partial_{y})^{k}\tilde{\psi}|_{\mathbb{R}}=\partial_{x}^{k}\psi\quad\mbox{for any}\ k\in\mathbb{N}_{0},

and
for any
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

\big|\bar{\partial}\tilde{\psi}(z)\big|\leq C_{N}|y|^{N}\max_{k=0,\ldots,N+3}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}|\psi^{(k)}(x)|,

and for any
N,p\in\mathbb{N}

|\partial_{y}^{p}\tilde{\psi}(z)|\leq C_{N,p}|y|^{N}\max_{k=0,\ldots,N+p+2}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}|\psi^{(k)}(x)|.

This last bounds also holds for
N\in\mathbb{N}
and

p=0
under the additional assumption that

|y|\geq 1
or
{\rm dist}(x,{{\rm supp}}\,\psi)\geq\varepsilon_{0}
for some fixed
\varepsilon_{0}>0
.
Above, the constants
C_{N}
and
C_{N,p}
are independent of the function
\psi
.

Proof.

By the properties of the Fourier transform,
we check readily that
\tilde{\psi}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{C})
is an almost analytic extension of
\psi
whose derivatives satisfy the equalities of the statement.
Let us write

\chi_{0}(y_{1}):=e^{-2\pi y_{1}}\chi(y_{1})\quad\mbox{and}\quad\eta(y_{1}):=e^{-2\pi y_{1}}\chi^{\prime}(y_{1}).

We have

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}\tilde{\psi}(z)

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{2\pi iz\xi}\xi\chi^{\prime}(y\xi)\,\widehat{\psi}(\xi)d\xi=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{2\pi ix\xi}\eta(y\xi)\,\xi\widehat{\psi}(\xi)d\xi,

\displaystyle\tilde{\psi}(z)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{2\pi ix\xi}\chi_{0}(y\xi)\,\widehat{\psi}(\xi)d\xi,

\displaystyle\partial_{y}^{p}\tilde{\psi}(z)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{2\pi ix\xi}\chi_{0}^{(p)}(y\xi)\,\xi^{p}\widehat{\psi}(\xi)d\xi.

As
\eta(y_{1})
is supported in
\{|y_{1}|\in[2,4]\}
, we have

|\bar{\partial}\tilde{\psi}(z)|\leq|y|^{N}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\xi^{N+1}\widehat{\psi}(\xi)|d\xi.

Since
\psi
is compactly supported, we have

\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\xi^{N_{1}}\widehat{\psi}(\xi)|d\xi\lesssim\max_{k=N_{1},N_{1}+2}\sup_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}|\xi|^{k}|\widehat{\psi}(\xi)|\lesssim\max_{k=N_{1},N_{1}+2}\|\psi^{(k)}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R})}\lesssim\max_{k=N_{1},N_{1}+2}\|\psi^{(k)}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})},

yielding the first estimate.
The same argument gives the second estimate when
p>0
since
\chi_{0}^{(p)}
has the same support property as
\eta
.
For the case
p=0
,
we develop the Fourier transform:

\displaystyle\tilde{\psi}(z)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{2\pi i(z-w)\xi}\chi_{0}(y\xi)\psi(w)dwd\xi

\displaystyle=y^{N}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{2\pi i(z-w)\xi}\chi_{0}^{(N)}(y\xi)\frac{\psi(w)}{(-2\pi i(z-w))^{N}}dwd\xi,

after
N
integrations by parts in
\xi
.
Since
\chi^{(N)}(y_{1})
is supported in
\{|y_{1}|\in[2,4]\}
,
we readily estimate the integrand as long as
|z-w|
is bounded below away from zero.
This is the case when
|y|\geq 1
or
when
{\rm dist}(x,{{\rm supp}}\,\psi)\geq\varepsilon_{0}
.
∎

Corollary B.3
.

We continue with the hypothesis and notation of Lemma
B.2
.
We also fix the following:

•

a function
\chi_{1}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}:[0,1]
such that
\chi_{1}=1
on
[-1,1]
and
\chi_{1}=0
outside
[-2,2]
,

•

an interval
I\subset[-2,2]
and a function
\chi_{2}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}:[0,1])
such that
\chi_{2}(x)=1
if
{\rm dist}(x,I)\leq\varepsilon_{1}
and
\chi_{2}=0
outside
I^{\prime}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R},{\rm dist}(x,I)\leq 2\varepsilon_{1}\}
with
\varepsilon_{1}\in(0,0.1)
fixed.

We set

\phi(z):=\chi_{1}(y)\chi_{2}(x)\tilde{\psi}(z),\qquad z=x+iy.

This defines a smooth function
\phi:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}
supported in
I^{\prime}\times[-2,2]
.
Assuming that
{{\rm supp}}\,\psi\subset I
,

\phi

is an almost analytic extension of
\psi
that satisfies for any
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

\partial_{x}^{k}\phi|_{\mathbb{R}}=(-i\partial_{y})^{k}\phi|_{\mathbb{R}}=\partial_{x}^{k}\psi.

Moreover, for any
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

\big|\bar{\partial}\phi(z)\big|\leq C_{N}|y|^{N}\max_{k=0,\ldots,N+3}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}|\psi^{(k)}(x)|,

and for any
N,p\in\mathbb{N}

|\partial_{y}^{p}\phi(z)|\leq C_{N,p}|y|^{N}\max_{k=0,\ldots,N+p+2}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}|\psi^{(k)}(x)|.

Above, the constants
C_{N}
and
C_{N,p}
are independent of the function
\psi
.

Proof.

The statement follows from Lemma
B.2
and the computations:

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}\phi(z)

\displaystyle=\chi_{1}(y)\chi_{2}(x)\bar{\partial}\tilde{\psi}+\frac{1}{2}\chi^{\prime}_{1}(y)\chi_{2}(x)\tilde{\psi}(z)+\frac{i}{2}\chi_{1}(y)\chi_{2}^{\prime}(x)\tilde{\psi}(z),

\displaystyle\partial_{y}^{p}\phi(z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{p_{1}=0}^{p}\binom{p}{p_{1}}\chi_{1}(x)\chi_{2}^{(p_{1})}(y)\partial_{y}^{p-p_{1}}\tilde{\psi}(z).

∎

We are now ready to prove Proposition
B.1
:

Proof of Proposition
B.1
.

Applying Corollary
B.3

with
I=[-2,2]
gives the property for a function
\psi
with compact support in
[-3/2,3/2]
. Hence,
we may assume that
\psi
is supported outside
[-1,1]
.
Let us show the case of
{{\rm supp}}\,\psi\subset[1,+\infty)
, the case of
(-\infty,-1]
following readily after sign modifications.

We fix a dyadic decomposition
(\eta_{j})
of
[1/2,+\infty)
, that is,
\eta_{0}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\frac{1}{2},2)
with

\sum_{j=-1}^{\infty}\eta_{j}(\lambda)=1\ \mbox{for all}\ \lambda\geq 1/2,\quad\mbox{where}\quad\eta_{j}(\lambda):=\eta_{0}(2^{-j}\lambda).

As
{{\rm supp}}\,\psi\subset[1,+\infty)
,
we may write for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}

\psi(\lambda)=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{jm^{\prime}}\psi_{j}(2^{-j}\lambda),\quad\mbox{where}\quad\psi_{j}(\lambda)\coloneqq 2^{-jm^{\prime}}\psi(2^{j}\lambda)\eta_{0}(\lambda).

We observe that
\psi_{j}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\frac{1}{2},2)
satisfies for all
j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and any
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

(B.1)

\sup_{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}}|\phi_{j}^{(k)}(\lambda)|\lesssim_{k}\|\psi\|_{\mathcal{G}^{m^{\prime}},k},

with an implicit constant independent of
j
.

Let
\phi_{j}
be the almost analytic extension constructed in Corollary
B.3
for
I=[1/2,2]
and
\varepsilon_{1}=0,001
.
We set at least formally:

(B.2)

\tilde{\psi}(z):=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{jm^{\prime}}\phi_{j}(2^{-j}z).

This sum is in fact locally finite.
Indeed, let us write
z=x+iy
.
Each term in the sum in (
B.2
)
vanishes when
x<1/4
, so we may assume
x\geq 1/4
. We set
j_{0}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that

x\sim 2^{j_{0}}
, in the sense that
j_{0}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
is the
largest non-negative integer smaller than
\ln x/\ln 2
when
\ln x/\ln 2>0
,
and
j_{0}=0
otherwise.
We have

\tilde{\psi}(z):=\sum_{j=\max(0,j_{0}-1)}^{j_{0}+1}\phi_{j}(2^{-j}z).

This finite sum vanishes when
|y|>2^{j_{0}+2}
, implying the stated support property for
\tilde{\psi}
.

As the sum over
j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
is locally finite, we check readily that the function
\tilde{\psi}
is smooth.
It follows from
Corollary
B.3
that it is an almost analytic extension of
\psi
with derivatives satisfying the stated equalities and supported in
\{\text{\rm Re}\,z\geq 1/4\}
.
We also have with
\text{\rm Re}\,z=x\geq 1/4
and
j_{0}
as above,

\displaystyle\big|\partial_{y}^{p}\tilde{\psi}(z)\big|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=\max(0,j_{0}-1)}^{j_{0}+1}2^{j(m^{\prime}-p)}|(\partial_{y}^{p}\phi_{j})(2^{-j}z)|

\displaystyle\lesssim_{N,p}|y|^{N}\sum_{j=\max(0,j_{0}-1)}^{j_{0}+1}2^{j(m^{\prime}-p)}\max_{k=0,\ldots,N+p+2}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}|\psi_{j}^{(k)}(x)|

\displaystyle\lesssim_{N,p}\|\psi\|_{\mathcal{G}^{m^{\prime}},N+p+2}|y|^{N}(1+|z|)^{m^{\prime}-p},

by (
B.1
) and
since
(1+|z|)\sim 2^{j_{0}}
.

It remains to show the stated integral estimate.
We have for any
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{C}}\big|\bar{\partial}\tilde{\psi}(z)\big|\left(\frac{1+|z|}{|y|}\right)^{N}L(dz)\leq\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{j(m^{\prime}+1-N)}\int_{\mathbb{C}}\big|\bar{\partial}\phi_{j}(z)\big|\left(\frac{1+2^{j}|z|}{|y|}\right)^{N}L(dz)

\displaystyle\qquad\lesssim_{N}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{j(m^{\prime}+1)}\int_{|z|\leq 10}\frac{\big|\bar{\partial}\phi_{j}(z)\big|}{|y|^{N}}L(dz)

\displaystyle\qquad\lesssim_{N}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{j(m^{\prime}+1)}\max_{k=0,\ldots,N+3}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}|\phi_{j}^{(k)}(x)|\lesssim_{N}\|\psi\|_{\mathcal{G}^{m^{\prime}},N+3}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{j(m^{\prime}+1)},

having used the properties of
\phi_{j}

from Corollary
B.3
and in (
B.1
) . The last sum is convergent since
m^{\prime}<-1
.
∎
